<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that every compact connected Kähler manifold of positive complex dimension containing no nonconstant entire curve has ample canonical bundle and is projective. This resolves positively Kobayashi's canonical-ampleness conjecture in the smooth compact Kähler category.

- **claim-main-canonical-ampleness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $X$ be a compact connected Kähler manifold of positive complex dimension. If every holomorphic map $\mathbb C\to X$ is constant, then $K_X$ is ample. In particular, a positive tensor power of $K_X$ defines a holomorphic embedding of $X$ into a complex projective space.
  - Explanation: On a compact connected Kähler manifold of positive dimension, the absence of nonconstant entire curves forces the canonical bundle to be ample, so a tensor power embeds the manifold in projective space.
- **claim-green-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-pre-green)): If $u$ is smooth on $\mathbb D$, then, for $0<r<1$, $$\begin{aligned} \langle u\rangle_r-u(0) &=\frac2\pi\int_{\|z\|<r}\log\frac r{\|z\|}\, \partial_z\partial_{\bar z}u\,\mathrm dS, \label{eq:green-poisson}\\ \frac1\pi\int_{\|z\|<r}\partial_z\partial_{\bar z}u\,\mathrm dS &=\frac r2\langle\partial_r u\rangle_r. \label{eq:green-area} \end{aligned}$$ If in addition $u$ is continuous on $\overline\mathbb D$ and $\partial_z\partial_{\bar z}u\in L^a(\mathbb D)$ for some $a>1$, then Equation <a href="#eq:green-poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:green-poisson">[eq:green-poisson]</a> passes to $r=1$.
  - Explanation: Planar Green identities relate circle means, the central value and radial derivatives of a smooth function to integrals of its mixed second derivative, with an extension to the full disc under mild regularity.
- **claim-brody-derivative-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-ex-brody)): There is a constant $D>0$, depending only on $(X,k)$, such that every holomorphic map $f\colon\mathbb D\to X$ satisfies $$d(f)=\|f'(0)\|_k^2\leq D.$$ Consequently, $$\|f'(z)\|_k\leq\frac{\sqrt D}{1-\|z\|},\qquad z\in\mathbb D. \label{eq:brody-derivative}$$ Every sequence of such maps has a subsequence converging uniformly on compact subsets of $\mathbb D$ to a holomorphic map into $X$. In local target coordinates, all derivatives converge uniformly on smaller compact subsets as well.
  - Explanation: Hyperbolicity of the compact target gives a uniform bound on the central derivative of every disc, hence an interior derivative estimate and normality of families of discs.
- **claim-maximizing-disc-exists** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ex-maximizer)): The functional in Equation <a href="#eq:disc-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:disc-functional">[eq:disc-functional]</a> has finite supremum, attained by a finite-area holomorphic disc centered at $x$.
  - Explanation: The penalized disc functional has a finite supremum and it is attained by a finite-area disc with prescribed center.
- **claim-dilation-area-tail** (bound, [source](#stmt-ex-dilation)): If $f$ is a maximizer in Proposition <a href="#ex:maximizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:maximizer">4</a>, then, for $0<r<1$, $$\begin{split} 0\leq\delta\bigl(A_h(f)-A_{h,r}(f)\bigr) &\leq(1-r^2)d(f) +2\int_r^1 A_{q,s}(f)\,\frac{ds}{s}. \end{split} \label{eq:tail}$$ In particular, $$A_q(f)\geq-d(f),\qquad A_k(f)-A_{k,r}(f)\leq C_f(1-r)\quad(1/2\leq r<1) \label{eq:extremal-area-tail}$$ for some finite constant $C_f$.
  - Explanation: Comparing a maximizer with its dilations yields a linear tail estimate for the boundary area and a lower bound on the area functional.
- **claim-boundary-regularity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ex-regularity)): Every disc maximizing the functional in Equation <a href="#eq:disc-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:disc-functional">[eq:disc-functional]</a> extends continuously to $\overline\mathbb D$ and belongs to $W^{1,p}(\mathbb D,X)$ for every $2<p<4$. In particular, it has continuous $W^{1,3}$ regularity up to the boundary. More precisely, there is a constant $C_f$ such that $$\|f'(z)\|_k\leq C_f(1-\|z\|)^{-1/2} \label{eq:boundary-derivative}$$ for $z$ sufficiently close to $\partial\mathbb D$.
  - Explanation: Every maximizing disc extends continuously to the closed disc and has Sobolev regularity in a range of exponents above two, with a quantitative derivative bound near the boundary.
- **claim-initial-holomorphic-frame** (theorem, [source](#stmt-var-initial-frame)): The bundle $f^*T^{1,0}X$ admits a holomorphic frame $e$ on $\mathbb D$ which extends to a continuous, nonsingular $W^{1,p}$ frame on $\overline\mathbb D$.
  - Explanation: The pulled-back tangent bundle has a holomorphic frame on the open disc that is continuous, nonsingular and Sobolev up to the boundary.
- **claim-boundary-unitary-frame** (theorem, [source](#stmt-var-frame)): Let $h$ be a smooth Hermitian metric on $X$. There exist a number $p_0$ with $2<p_0\le p$ and a holomorphic frame $B=(b_1,\ldots,b_n)$ of $f^*T^{1,0}X$ which is continuous, nonsingular, and $W^{1,p_0}$ on $\overline\mathbb D$, such that $$\label{var:boundary-unitary} B(z)^*h(f(z))B(z)=I, \qquad z\in\partial\mathbb D.$$
  - Explanation: A weighted Hardy-space factorization produces a holomorphic frame that is orthonormal for the metric on the boundary circle while keeping continuity and Sobolev regularity.
- **claim-centered-holomorphic-variation** (construction, [source](#stmt-var-spray)): Let $B$ and $p_0>2$ be as in Proposition <a href="#var:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:frame">9</a>, and set $x=f(0)$. For some $\eta>0$, there is a map $$F\colon\{t\in\mathbb C^n:\|t\|<\eta\}\times\overline\mathbb D\longrightarrow X$$ which is continuous on its domain and jointly holomorphic on $\{\|t\|<\eta\}\times\mathbb D$, and satisfies $$\label{var:spray-identities} F(0,z)=f(z),\qquad F(t,0)=x,\qquad \frac{\partial F}{\partial t_j}(0,z)=z b_j(z).$$ In fixed local target coordinates covering the graph of $f$, the maps $t\mapsto F(t,\cdot)$ are holomorphic with values in $W^{1,p_0}$ on the corresponding source patches. In particular every $F(t,\cdot)$ is a finite-area holomorphic disc centered at $x$.
  - Explanation: The boundary-unitary frame sections are integrated into a family of holomorphic discs with fixed center, depending holomorphically on parameters, with prescribed first-order variation.
- **claim-determinant-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-hes-determinant)): With these conventions, $$\label{eq:determinant-identity} \log\det N(0)=P_{R_h}(f).$$
  - Explanation: The logarithm of the determinant of the frame Gram matrix at the center equals the Poisson integral of the Ricci form along the disc.
- **claim-hessian-trace-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-hes-trace)): For every smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form $q$, $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal Ld(F_t)&=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(B(0)^*k(x)B(0)),\label{eq:hessian-central}\\ \mathcal LP_q(F_t)&=\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi} (\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h q)(f(e^{i\theta}))\,\mathrm d\theta,\label{eq:hessian-poisson}\\ \mathcal LA_h(F_t)&=n-A_{R_h}(f).\label{eq:hessian-area} \end{aligned}$$ Here $F_t$ means the disc $z\mapsto F(t,z)$, not a parameter derivative.
  - Explanation: The trace of the complex Hessian of the central, Poisson and area functionals along the centered variation is computed explicitly from boundary data and curvature.
- **claim-maximizing-inequality** (theorem, [source](#stmt-hes-maximum)): For a maximizer of $d+P_q-\delta A_h$ centered at $x$, the boundary-unitary frame satisfies $$\label{eq:maximum-inequality} \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(B(0)^*k(x)B(0)) +\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_hq)(f(e^{i\theta}))\,\mathrm d\theta \le\delta\bigl(n-A_{R_h}(f)\bigr).$$ It also satisfies Equation <a href="#eq:determinant-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:determinant-identity">[eq:determinant-identity]</a>, and $A_q(f)\ge-d(f)$.
  - Explanation: The second-derivative test at a maximizer gives an inequality bounding the frame trace and boundary form integral by a multiple of the area deficit.
- **claim-uniform-ricci-disc-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-nef-disc-bound)): There is a constant $C\geq 0$, depending only on the fixed metric $k$, such that $$P_{R_k}(f)\leq C \label{eq:nef-uniform-disc-bound}$$ for every finite-area holomorphic disc $f:\mathbb D\to X$.
  - Explanation: The Poisson Ricci functional is bounded above uniformly over all finite-area discs by a constant depending only on the fixed metric.
- **claim-bounded-canonical-potentials** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nef-bounded-potentials)): There is a bounded real-valued function $u$ on $X$ such that $$T=-R_k+i\partial\bar\partial u\geq0$$ is a closed positive $(1,1)$-current. In every local holomorphic frame $s_i$ of $\det T^{1,0}X$, put $a_i=\log\|s_i\|_k^2$. Then $a_i+u$ is plurisubharmonic and locally bounded. These are local weights of a singular Hermitian metric with nonnegative curvature on $K_X$.
  - Explanation: A disc envelope on the determinant bundle yields a bounded potential making the canonical curvature current positive, giving locally bounded plurisubharmonic weights.
- **claim-bounded-potential-smoothing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nef-bounded-smoothing)): Let $(M,k)$ be a compact Kähler manifold, let $\alpha$ be a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form on $M$, and let $u$ be a bounded real-valued function such that $\alpha+i\partial\bar\partial u\geq0$ in the sense of currents. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is a smooth real-valued function $u_\varepsilon$ on $M$ such that $$\alpha+i\partial\bar\partial u_\varepsilon\geq-\varepsilon k.$$
  - Explanation: A bounded potential with positive curvature current can be smoothed while losing arbitrarily little positivity.
- **claim-canonical-class-nef** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nef-nef)): For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is a smooth real-valued function $u_\varepsilon$ on $X$ such that $$-R_k+i\partial\bar\partial u_\varepsilon\geq-\varepsilon k.$$ Consequently $2\pi c_1(K_X)$ is nef.
  - Explanation: Smoothing the bounded canonical potential shows the canonical class is nef.
- **claim-uniform-volume-lower-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-volume-lower-bound)): For every smooth Kähler form $h$ on $X$ satisfying $R_h\ge-h$, one has $$\label{eq:uniform-volume-lower-bound} h^n\ge c_{n,D}\,k^n, \qquad c_{n,D}=e^{-D}\left(\frac{n}{2n+D}\right)^n>0.$$
  - Explanation: Every Kähler metric with Ricci form bounded below by minus the metric has top power bounded below by an explicit constant times the reference volume form.
- **claim-negative-monge-ampere** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-negative-cma)): Let $M$ be a compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n$, let $\gamma$ be a Kähler form on $M$, and let $\Omega$ be a smooth positive volume form. There is a smooth real function $w$ such that $$\gamma+i\partial\bar\partial w>0, \qquad (\gamma+i\partial\bar\partial w)^n=e^w\Omega.$$
  - Explanation: The negative-sign complex Monge–Ampère equation is solvable for any Kähler form and smooth positive volume form on a compact Kähler manifold.
- **claim-positive-top-intersection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-positive-top-intersection)): The canonical class has positive top self-intersection: $$\int_X c_1(K_X)^n>0.$$
  - Explanation: Combining nefness, the Monge–Ampère solutions and the uniform volume bound gives a strictly positive top self-intersection of the canonical class.
- **claim-nef-positive-implies-big** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-nef-big)): Let $L$ be a nef holomorphic line bundle on a compact connected Kähler manifold $(X,k)$ of dimension $n\ge1$. If $\int_Xc_1(L)^n>0$, then $L$ is big and $X$ is Moishezon.
  - Explanation: A nef line bundle with positive top self-intersection is big and the manifold is Moishezon, via control of the negative-index locus and Morse inequalities.
- **claim-moishezon-projectivity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-moishezon-projectivity)): A compact Moishezon manifold that admits a Kähler metric is projective.
  - Explanation: A Kähler Moishezon manifold is projective; this is a cited classical result.
- **claim-big-no-rational-curves-ample** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-no-rational-ample)): Let $X$ be a smooth projective variety over $\mathbb C$. If $K_X$ is big and $X$ contains no rational curves, then $K_X$ is ample.
  - Explanation: On a smooth projective variety, a big canonical bundle together with the absence of rational curves implies ampleness, via Kodaira's lemma and the log cone theorem.
- **claim-pluricanonical-freeness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-pluricanonical-freeness)): Let $X$ be a compact connected Brody-hyperbolic Kähler manifold of complex dimension $n\ge1$. Then $K_X^{\otimes m}$ is generated by its global sections for every integer $m\ge n+2$.
  - Explanation: For hyperbolic compact Kähler manifolds, high enough pluricanonical powers are globally generated, by applying a freeness theorem to the ample canonical bundle.
- **claim-finite-cover-product** (classification, [source](#stmt-cor-semialgebraic-product)): Let $X$ be a compact connected Brody-hyperbolic Kähler manifold of positive complex dimension. Suppose its ordinary universal cover is biholomorphic to a semialgebraic open subset of a complex projective variety, with openness in the ordinary complex topology. Then $X$ admits a connected finite étale cover biholomorphic to $$(D/\Gamma_0)\times F,$$ where $D$ is a bounded symmetric domain, $\Gamma_0$ is a discrete group of biholomorphisms acting freely, properly discontinuously and cocompactly on $D$, and $F$ is a simply connected compact Brody-hyperbolic projective manifold. Either $D$ or $F$ may be a point.
  - Explanation: Under a semialgebraic realization of the universal cover, a finite étale cover splits as a compact quotient of a bounded symmetric domain times a simply connected hyperbolic projective manifold.
- **claim-radius-comparison** (bound, [source](#stmt-nef-radius-comparison)): Let $\psi$ be plurisubharmonic on a domain in $\mathbb C^n$. Suppose that all closed balls $\overline B(x,R)$ with centers in a fixed set $E$ are contained in the domain, and that $m\leq\psi\leq M$ on their union. Put $$S_x(r)=\sup_{\|z-x\|\leq r}\psi(z),\qquad 0<r\leq R.$$ For every $A>1$ and $Ar<R$, $$0\leq S_x(Ar)-S_x(r) \leq\frac{(M-m)\log A}{\log(R/r)},\qquad x\in E. \label{eq:nef-radius-comparison}$$ For each sufficiently small fixed $r$, the function $x\mapsto S_x(r)$ is plurisubharmonic on its open domain of definition.
  - Explanation: For bounded plurisubharmonic functions, spherical suprema at radii differing by a fixed factor differ by an amount that decays logarithmically, and fixed-radius suprema remain plurisubharmonic.

        "anchor": "stmt-hes-maximum",
        "exact_quote": "For a maximizer of $d+P_q-\\delta A_h$ centered at $x$, the boundary-unitary frame satisfies $$\\label{eq:maximum-inequality}\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(B(0)^*k(x)B(0))\n +\\frac1{2\\pi}\\int_0^{2\\pi}(\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}_hq)(f(e^{i\\theta}))\\,\\mathrm d\\theta\n \\le\\delta\\bigl(n-A_{R_h}(f)\\bigr).$$ It also satisfies Equation <a href=\"#eq:determinant-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:determinant-identity\">[eq:determinant-identity]</a>, and $A_q(f)\\ge-d(f)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-051.1"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Brody hyperbolic** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A complex manifold is *Brody hyperbolic* if every holomorphic map $\mathbb C\to X$ is constant.
- **ample line bundle** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A line bundle is *ample* if some positive tensor power has global sections defining a holomorphic embedding into projective space.
- **nef line bundle** ([source](#pm-h-23)): A holomorphic line bundle is nef if, for every $\varepsilon>0$, it admits a smooth Hermitian metric whose real curvature is at least $-\varepsilon k$.
- **finite-area disc** ([source](#sec:preliminaries)): The disc has *finite area* if $\int_\mathbb Dk(f',f')\,\mathrm dS<\infty$.
- **Moishezon manifold** ([source](#eq:morse-bigness-criterion)): A compact complex manifold is Moishezon when its field of meromorphic functions has transcendence degree $n$.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-brody-central-derivative | squared central derivative norm d(f) | ≤ | $D$ | D depends only on the manifold and the metric |
| q-interior-derivative-growth | disc derivative norm | ≤ | $\frac{\sqrt D}{1-\|z\|}$ | points z of the open unit disc |
| q-area-lower-bound | area functional of a maximizer | ≥ | $-d(f)$ | fixed Kähler form, closed real form and positive penalty |
| q-boundary-derivative-rate | derivative norm of a maximizer near the boundary | ≤ | $C_f(1-\|z\|)^{-1/2}$ | constant depends on the individual disc; valid close to the boundary circle |
| q-determinant-identity | log determinant of the frame Gram matrix at the center | = | $P_{R_h}(f)$ | fixed Kähler metric h |
| q-hessian-area-trace | complex Hessian trace of the area functional | = | $n-A_{R_h}(f)$ | parameter derivatives at the zero parameter |
| q-ricci-disc-bound | Poisson Ricci functional of a disc | ≤ | $C$ | C depends only on the fixed metric |
| q-volume-lower-constant | volume comparison constant | = | $e^{-D}\left(\frac{n}{2n+D}\right)^n$ | depends only on the dimension and the derivative constant |
| q-radius-comparison-bound | difference of suprema at radii differing by a fixed factor | ≤ | $\frac{(M-m)\log A}{\log(R/r)}$ | centers in the fixed set |
| q-freeness-threshold | pluricanonical exponent for global generation | ≥ | $n+2$ | integers m |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **rs-brody-bound** [claim](#stmt-ex-brody) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-brody): Rescaling contradiction and Arzelà–Ascoli compactness -> uniform central derivative bound and normality
- **rs-maximizer** [claim](#stmt-ex-maximizer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-maximizer): Outer-annulus penalty control, compactness and Fatou semicontinuity -> existence of a maximizing disc
- **rs-dilation** [claim](#stmt-ex-dilation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-dilation): Compare the maximizer with its dilations -> area tail estimate
- **rs-regularity** [claim](#stmt-ex-regularity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-regularity): Mean-value estimate with area tail, radial integration, dyadic annuli -> continuity and Sobolev regularity up to the boundary
- **rs-initial-frame** [claim](#stmt-var-initial-frame) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-initial-frame): Cauchy transform gauge solving and Oka–Grauert triviality -> boundary-regular holomorphic frame
- **rs-boundary-frame** [claim](#stmt-var-frame) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-frame): Hardy-space spectral factorization with boundary Sobolev regularity -> boundary-unitary frame
- **rs-spray** [claim](#stmt-var-spray) / [proof](#proof-stmt-var-spray): Fiber coordinates and complex Banach implicit function theorem with fixed center -> centered holomorphic variation family
- **rs-determinant** [claim](#stmt-hes-determinant) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hes-determinant): Harmonicity of the log modulus of the determinant and Green identity -> determinant identity
- **rs-trace** [claim](#stmt-hes-trace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hes-trace): Differentiate under the integrals, local Hessian identity, weak boundary limit -> trace identities
- **rs-maximum** [claim](#stmt-hes-maximum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-hes-maximum): Second-derivative test at a local maximum -> maximizing inequality
- **rs-disc-bound** [claim](#stmt-nef-disc-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nef-disc-bound): Arithmetic–geometric mean inequality and vanishing penalty limit -> uniform Ricci-disc bound
- **rs-potentials** [claim](#stmt-nef-bounded-potentials) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nef-bounded-potentials): Poletsky–Rosay envelope on the determinant bundle minus its zero section -> bounded positive canonical potential
- **rs-radius-comparison** [claim](#stmt-nef-radius-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nef-radius-comparison): Convexity in the logarithm of the radius -> fixed-factor radius comparison
- **rs-smoothing** [claim](#stmt-nef-bounded-smoothing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nef-radius-comparison-2): Regularize local suprema, compare on overlaps, glue by a regularized maximum -> smoothing with small loss of positivity
- **rs-nef** [claim](#stmt-nef-nef) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nef-nef): Apply smoothing to the bounded canonical potential -> nefness of the canonical class
- **rs-volume** [claim](#stmt-prop-volume-lower-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-volume-lower-bound): Maximizer with negative form and unit penalty, then determinant quotient -> uniform volume lower bound
- **rs-cma** [claim](#stmt-thm-negative-cma) / [proof](#stmt-thm-negative-cma): Cited Aubin–Yau existence theorem -> solutions of the negative-sign Monge–Ampère equation
- **rs-intersection** [claim](#stmt-prop-positive-top-intersection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-positive-top-intersection): Twisted Monge–Ampère metrics in perturbed classes, volume bound, polynomial in the perturbation -> positive top self-intersection
- **rs-big** [claim](#stmt-lem-nef-big) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-nef-big): Bound the mass of the negative-index locus and apply the Morse bigness criterion -> bigness and Moishezon property
- **rs-moishezon** [claim](#stmt-thm-moishezon-projectivity) / [proof](#stmt-thm-moishezon-projectivity): Cited projectivity theorem -> projectivity
- **rs-ample** [claim](#stmt-prop-no-rational-ample) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-no-rational-ample): Kodaira lemma, klt perturbation, log cone theorem, Kleiman criterion -> ampleness of the canonical bundle
- **rs-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-no-rational-ample-2): Hyperbolicity excludes rational curves; combine projectivity, bigness and ampleness -> main theorem
- **rs-freeness** [claim](#stmt-cor-pluricanonical-freeness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-pluricanonical-freeness): Apply a freeness theorem to the ample canonical bundle -> global generation of pluricanonical powers
- **rs-semialgebraic** [claim](#stmt-cor-semialgebraic-product) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-semialgebraic-product): Cited cover classification, finiteness of automorphisms, deck group analysis -> finite-cover product decomposition

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-uniform-volume-lower-bound**: For every smooth Kähler form h satisfying the Ricci lower bound, the volume form of h dominates a fixed positive multiple of the volume form of k. The constant depends only on the dimension and on the Brody derivative constant D, never on h.
- **inv-central-derivative-bound**: A single constant D, depending only on the compact manifold and the fixed metric, bounds the squared norm of the central derivative of every holomorphic disc.
- **inv-canonical-ampleness**: On a compact connected Kähler manifold of positive dimension, absence of nonconstant entire curves forces the canonical bundle to be ample.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-uniform-disc-constants**: Treating the regularity constants of maximizing discs as uniform bounds and reading them as computable convergence or complexity guarantees. -> Only the derivative constant D is uniform; the constants for each maximizing disc depend on the chosen metric, form and penalty parameter, so no uniform rate follows.
- **anti-entire-curves-present**: Concluding that the canonical bundle is ample for a compact Kähler manifold that carries a nonconstant entire curve. -> Without the hypothesis the derivative bound D is not available and the argument gives no ampleness.
- **anti-freeness-low-exponent**: Asserting global generation of the pluricanonical bundle for exponents below the stated threshold. -> The corollary only certifies exponents at or above dimension plus two; smaller exponents are not covered.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main results are existential and analytic: a maximizing disc is obtained by a compactness argument (the supremum is attained by a finite-area holomorphic disc), frames come from Hardy-space factorization and a Banach implicit function theorem, and ampleness follows from cited existence theorems. No finite procedure with explicit steps computes an object from finite input, so no runnable reference implementation or finite oracle can certify these statements.

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-compact-kahler-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The main theorem and every estimate are proved only for a compact connected Kähler manifold of positive complex dimension; compactness and the Kähler metric are used throughout the disc argument. Blocked use: ['Applying the ampleness conclusion to a noncompact manifold', 'Applying the ampleness conclusion to a compact complex manifold that is not Kähler']
- **lim-no-entire-curves** (hypothesis): The derivative bound that starts the whole argument needs every holomorphic map from the complex line into the manifold to be constant. Without it, the bound on discs and the ampleness conclusion are not available. Blocked use: ['Claiming canonical ampleness for a manifold that carries a nonconstant entire curve', 'Using the derivative constant D when hyperbolicity has not been verified']
- **lim-nonuniform-disc-constants** (edge_case): The regularity constants of maximizing discs need not be uniform in the metric, the closed form or the penalty parameter. Only the constant D depends on the fixed metric and the manifold alone. Blocked use: ['Reading per-disc regularity constants as uniform or computable convergence guarantees', 'Deriving rates or complexity for the disc optimization']
- **lim-maximizer-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): A maximizing disc is obtained from a maximizing sequence by compactness and Fatou semicontinuity, so the paper gives no finite procedure that produces it. Blocked use: ['Implementing the extremal disc as an algorithm with a stated cost', 'Presenting the maximizer as an explicitly computable object']
- **lim-regularity-exponent-range** (formal_scope): Sobolev regularity of a maximizer is proved only for exponents strictly between two and four, with a derivative bound valid near the boundary circle. Blocked use: ['Assuming Sobolev regularity of a maximizing disc for exponents of four or more', 'Assuming the boundary derivative bound holds in the interior with the same constant']
- **lim-frame-needs-p-above-two** (hypothesis): The boundary frames and centered variations are built for discs of Sobolev class with exponent above two, which gives continuity and the algebra property; no extension across the boundary circle is assumed or produced. Blocked use: ['Applying the frame and spray construction to a disc with only exponent at most two', 'Assuming the maps extend holomorphically across the circle']
- **lim-volume-ricci-condition** (hypothesis): The uniform lower bound on the volume form holds only for smooth Kähler forms whose Ricci form is bounded below by minus the metric. Blocked use: ['Applying the volume bound to Kähler metrics violating the Ricci lower bound']
- **lim-cma-existence-cited** (formal_scope): Solvability of the negative-sign Monge–Ampère equation is quoted from an existence theorem, so no explicit solution or estimate on it is supplied. Blocked use: ['Using the Monge–Ampère solutions as computed metrics with explicit potential bounds']
- **lim-freeness-threshold** (edge_case): Global generation of pluricanonical powers is certified only for exponents at or above dimension plus two. Blocked use: ['Asserting global generation for smaller exponents']
- **lim-semialgebraic-cover** (hypothesis): The finite-cover product decomposition needs the universal cover to be biholomorphic to a semialgebraic open subset of a complex projective variety, with openness in the ordinary topology. Blocked use: ['Applying the product decomposition to general hyperbolic manifolds without this semialgebraic realization']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that every compact connected Kähler manifold of positive complex dimension containing no nonconstant entire curve has ample canonical bundle and is projective. This resolves positively Kobayashi's canonical-ampleness conjecture in the smooth compact Kähler category.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A complex manifold is *Brody hyperbolic* if every holomorphic map $\mathbb C\to X$ is constant. On a compact complex manifold, Brody’s theorem identifies this condition with Kobayashi hyperbolicity, the nondegeneracy of the intrinsic pseudodistance defined by holomorphic disc chains (Brody 1978; Kobayashi 1970) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Brody1978](#ref-Brody1978), [Kobayashi1970](#ref-Kobayashi1970)]. We use “hyperbolic” in this sense throughout.

For a complex manifold of dimension $n$, the canonical bundle $K_X=\bigwedge^n(T^{1,0}X)^*$ is the line bundle of holomorphic $n$-forms. A line bundle is *ample* if some positive tensor power has global sections defining a holomorphic embedding into projective space. Kobayashi’s canonical-ampleness conjecture predicts that the absence of nonconstant entire curves forces $K_X$ to be ample on a compact Kähler manifold. It therefore connects a restriction on one-dimensional holomorphic maps with both positivity of top-degree forms and projective algebraicity. We prove this implication in every positive dimension.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $X$ be a compact connected Kähler manifold of positive complex dimension. If every holomorphic map $\mathbb C\to X$ is constant, then $K_X$ is ample. In particular, a positive tensor power of $K_X$ defines a holomorphic embedding of $X$ into a complex projective space.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and the main difficulty

Kobayashi’s theory supplies the intrinsic metric framework (Kobayashi 1970) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Kobayashi1970](#ref-Kobayashi1970)]; the canonical-ampleness question and its relationship with curvature are discussed in (Diverio 2020) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Diverio2020](#ref-Diverio2020)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives a positive resolution in the smooth compact Kähler category. A major preceding advance is the theorem of Wu and Yau: negative holomorphic sectional curvature of a Kähler metric forces canonical ampleness on a smooth projective manifold (Wu and Yau 2016b) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[WuYau2016](#ref-WuYau2016)]. Tosatti and Yang extended that implication to compact Kähler manifolds without assuming projectivity (Tosatti and Yang 2017) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[TosattiYang2017](#ref-TosattiYang2017)]. Diverio–Trapani extended the conclusion to quasi-negative holomorphic sectional curvature: the curvature is nonpositive everywhere and strictly negative in every nonzero tangent direction at one point (Diverio and Trapani 2019) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DiverioTrapani2019](#ref-DiverioTrapani2019)]. Wu–Yau subsequently gave another proof covering both the negative and quasi-negative cases (Wu and Yau 2016a) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[WuYauRemark2016](#ref-WuYauRemark2016)]. Broder–Stanfield allow the negatively curved metric $\omega$ to be Hermitian and pluriclosed, meaning $i\partial\bar\partial\omega=0$, while retaining the ambient Kähler hypothesis (Broder and Stanfield 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BroderStanfield2026](#ref-BroderStanfield2026)]. These metric hypotheses give curvature inequalities unavailable from the absence of entire curves alone. The present proof begins instead with the derivative bounds supplied by compact hyperbolicity.

A related line of work uses Gromov’s Kähler hyperbolicity (Gromov 1991) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Gromov1991](#ref-Gromov1991)]: a Kähler form pulls back to the exterior derivative of a bounded one-form on the universal covering. Building on Gromov’s general-type result, Chen and Yang proved canonical ampleness under this hypothesis (Chen and Yang 2018, Definitions 2.2 and 2.7, Theorem 2.11) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[ChenYang2018](#ref-ChenYang2018)]. This condition is distinct from Brody hyperbolicity (Chen and Yang 2018, Corollary 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ChenYang2018](#ref-ChenYang2018)].

Recent work also establishes a weaker positivity conclusion under the absence of rational curves. A line bundle is *nef* if, for every $\varepsilon>0$, it admits a smooth Hermitian metric whose curvature is bounded below by $-\varepsilon\omega$, where $\omega$ is any fixed background Kähler form. Ou’s characterization of uniruled compact Kähler manifolds (Ou 2025, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Ou2025](#ref-Ou2025)] shows that, in the absence of rational curves, $K_X$ is pseudoeffective: its first Chern class contains a closed positive current. Ou’s theorem also supplies the lower-dimensional hypothesis in Cao–Höring’s rational-curve theorem (Cao and Höring 2020, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[CaoHoring2020](#ref-CaoHoring2020)]. The latter then shows that failure of nefness would produce a rational curve. Thus nefness is already known under this weaker hypothesis. The further issue for ampleness is positive canonical volume. We retain a direct disc proof of nefness because it produces bounded canonical potentials and uses the same Hessian identity as our volume estimate.

Extremal holomorphic discs have long connected intrinsic complex geometry with analytic estimates, notably in Lempert’s theory on convex domains (Lempert 1981) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Lempert1981](#ref-Lempert1981)]. The extremal problem used here includes an area penalty on discs in a compact target. Its difficulty is at the boundary: compact convergence does not by itself justify variations of an integral over the whole unit disc. We first obtain boundary Sobolev regularity from maximality. We then construct a holomorphic frame normalized on the boundary and integrate the needed infinitesimal sections into actual holomorphic families that keep the center fixed. These steps make one Hessian calculation available for two different positivity estimates.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The analytic argument

Put $n=\dim_{\mathbb C}X$, let $\mathbb D=\{|z|<1\}$, and fix a Kähler metric $k$. A disc $f:\mathbb D\to X$ has finite area if the integral of $k(f'(z),f'(z))$ over $\mathbb D$ is finite. Compact hyperbolicity bounds the squared $k$-norm of the central derivative of every holomorphic disc by a constant $D$. For a closed real $(1,1)$-form $q$, evaluated through its associated Hermitian tensor, use the area and Poisson integrals $$A_q(f)=\frac1\pi\int_\mathbb Dq(f',f')\,\mathrm dS,
 \qquad
 P_q(f)=\frac2\pi\int_\mathbb D\log\frac1{|z|}\,q(f',f')\,\mathrm dS,$$ where $\mathrm dS$ is Euclidean area. We maximize $$k(f'(0),f'(0))+P_q(f)-\delta A_h(f)$$ over finite-area discs with a fixed center, where $h$ is a Kähler metric and $\delta>0$. The negative area term gives compactness. Comparing a maximizing disc with its dilations then gives a linear boundary-area tail bound. This improves its regularity to continuity on the closed disc and $W^{1,p}$ for some $p>2$.

The main analytic work is to make boundary-normalized variations at such an extremum legitimate. A weighted Hardy-space construction, in the tradition of matrix spectral factorization (Wiener and Masani 1957; Helson and Lowdenslager 1958) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[WienerMasani1957](#ref-WienerMasani1957), [HelsonLowdenslager1958](#ref-HelsonLowdenslager1958)], gives a holomorphic frame $B=(b_1,\ldots,b_n)$ of the pulled-back tangent bundle with $B^*hB=I$ on the boundary. We retain enough Sobolev regularity to realize the sections $zb_j(z)$ as derivatives of actual center-fixed holomorphic families $F_t$ with $F_0=f$. Let $\mathcal L$ denote the sum of the complex second derivatives in these $n$ parameter directions, evaluated at $t=0$. The trace of the complex Hessian of the area functional is then $$\mathcal LA_h(F_t)=n-A_{R_h}(f),
 \qquad R_h=-i\partial\bar\partial\log\det h,
 \qquad dd^c=i\partial\bar\partial.$$ Its proof uses an explicit weak boundary limit; it does not assume that the maximizing disc extends holomorphically across the circle. The frame and variation constructions apply to boundary-regular holomorphic discs independently of the global hyperbolicity hypothesis, which is used to produce those discs.

Two choices of parameters exploit this identity:

<div class="center">

| Parameters                          | Result                            |
|:------------------------------------|:----------------------------------|
| $h=k$, $q=R_k$, $\delta\downarrow0$ | Uniform upper bound for $P_{R_k}$ |
| $q=-h$, $\delta=1$, $R_h\ge-h$      | Uniform lower bound for $h^n/k^n$ |

</div>

<a id="eq:intro-volume"></a>
The first bound holds on all finite-area discs. The Poletsky–Rosay disc envelope (Poletsky 1991; Rosay 2003; Drinovec Drnovšek and Forstnerič 2012) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Poletsky1991](#ref-Poletsky1991), [Rosay2003](#ref-Rosay2003), [DrinovecForstneric2012](#ref-DrinovecForstneric2012)] on $\det T^{1,0}X=K_X^*$ with its zero section removed then gives bounded local plurisubharmonic weights for $K_X$. We include a direct smoothing argument proving that $K_X$ is nef. Second, $q=-h$ gives the pointwise estimate $$\label{eq:intro-volume}
 h^n\ge e^{-D}\left(\frac{n}{2n+D}\right)^n k^n
 \quad\text{whenever }R_h\ge-h.$$ This estimate is uniform over the indicated metrics, even though the auxiliary maximizing discs and their regularity constants need not be.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### From the estimates to ampleness

Following the Monge–Ampère and volume strategy used by Wu and Yau (Wu and Yau 2016b) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[WuYau2016](#ref-WuYau2016)], the negative-sign Aubin–Yau equation produces Kähler metrics $h_t$, for $t>0$, in the classes $2\pi c_1(K_X)+t[k]$ with $R_{h_t}=-h_t+t k$. Equation <a href="#eq:intro-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-volume">[eq:intro-volume]</a> gives a positive lower bound for their volumes. Passing to the limit in cohomology as $t\downarrow0$ proves $\int_X c_1(K_X)^n>0$.

The remaining steps use established results with their usual hypotheses. Holomorphic Morse inequalities make the nef canonical bundle *big*, meaning that its sections have maximal, $n$-dimensional asymptotic growth. The resulting sections make $X$ Moishezon, that is, its meromorphic function field has transcendence degree $n$. A compact Kähler Moishezon manifold is projective. Finally, the absence of rational curves and the log cone theorem upgrade bigness to ampleness. The final implication is recorded in (Diverio and Trapani 2019, Lemma 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[DiverioTrapani2019](#ref-DiverioTrapani2019)]; see also (Diverio 2020, Lemma 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Diverio2020](#ref-Diverio2020)]. All the new estimates are proved below; the standard existence, envelope, and positivity theorems are stated at the points where they are used.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes the curvature, Green-function, and disc-functional conventions. Section <a href="#sec:extremal-discs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:extremal-discs">3</a> constructs maximizing discs and proves their boundary regularity. Section <a href="#var:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:section">4</a> builds the boundary-unitary frames and centered holomorphic variations, and Section <a href="#sec:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hessian">5</a> establishes the determinant and Hessian identities. Their first use, in Section <a href="#sec:nefness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nefness">6</a>, produces bounded canonical potentials and proves nefness; their second use, in Section <a href="#sec:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:volume">7</a>, gives the uniform volume estimate. Section <a href="#sec:positive-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:positive-intersection">8</a> turns that estimate into positive top self-intersection, and Section <a href="#sec:ampleness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:ampleness">9</a> completes the passage to bigness, projectivity, and ampleness. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">10</a> applies the result to pluricanonical sections and semialgebraic universal covers. Appendix <a href="#app:smoothing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:smoothing">11</a> gives the bounded-potential smoothing argument used in Section <a href="#sec:nefness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nefness">6</a>.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Conventions and disc functionals

Throughout, $X$ is a compact connected Kähler manifold of complex dimension $n\ge1$, with no nonconstant entire curves, and $k$ is a fixed Kähler metric. This section fixes the signs and normalizations used in the disc estimates. We write $dd^c=i\partial\bar\partial$ and identify a Hermitian tensor with its real $(1,1)$-form so that the tensor associated with a real-valued potential $\phi$ has quadratic form $$\sum_{i,j}\partial_i\partial_{\bar j}\phi\,
 v^i\overline{v^j},$$ and associated form $i\partial\bar\partial\phi$. In matrix formulas, vectors are columns and their squared norm is written $v^*hv$. In particular, if the columns of $B$ are tangent vectors, their Gram matrix is $B^*hB$.

For a Kähler form $h$, set <a id="eq:ricci-convention"></a>$$\label{eq:ricci-convention}
 R_h=-i\partial\bar\partial\log\det h.$$ This globally defined form represents $2\pi c_1(T^{1,0}X)$; hence $[-R_h]=2\pi c_1(K_X)$. Inequalities of real $(1,1)$-forms are understood as inequalities of their Hermitian tensors. The form $h^n$ is used without a factor $n!$; determinant ratios are consequently ratios of these volume forms.

Write $\mathbb D=\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|<1\}$ and let $\mathrm dS$ denote Euclidean area. For a holomorphic map $f:\mathbb D\to X$, put $$f'(z)=\mathrm df_z(\partial_z),\qquad d(f)=k(f'(0),f'(0)).$$ The disc has *finite area* if $\int_\mathbb Dk(f',f')\,\mathrm dS<\infty$. Since $X$ is compact, this condition is independent of the smooth Hermitian metric used to define it. For a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form $q$ and a finite-area disc, define <a id="eq:disc-functionals"></a>$$\label{eq:disc-functionals}
 A_q(f)=\frac1\pi\int_\mathbb Dq(f',f')\,\mathrm dS,
 \qquad
 P_q(f)=\frac2\pi\int_\mathbb D\log\frac1{|z|}\,q(f',f')\,\mathrm dS.$$ Both are finite: the weight is bounded away from the center, and the integrand apart from the weight is smooth near the center. We write $A_{q,r}(f)$ for the same area integral restricted to $|z|<r$.

For a scalar function on a circle, use the normalized mean $$\langle u\rangle_r=\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}u(re^{i\theta})\,\mathrm d\theta.$$ The following normalization will be useful repeatedly.

<a id="stmt-pre-green"></a>
<div id="pre:green" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:green-poisson"></a>
<a id="eq:green-area"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Green identities). *If $u$ is smooth on $\mathbb D$, then, for $0<r<1$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle u\rangle_r-u(0)
 &=\frac2\pi\int_{|z|<r}\log\frac r{|z|}\,
       \partial_z\partial_{\bar z}u\,\mathrm dS,
       \label{eq:green-poisson}\\
 \frac1\pi\int_{|z|<r}\partial_z\partial_{\bar z}u\,\mathrm dS
 &=\frac r2\langle\partial_r u\rangle_r.
       \label{eq:green-area}
\end{aligned}$$ If in addition $u$ is continuous on $\overline\mathbb D$ and $\partial_z\partial_{\bar z}u\in L^a(\mathbb D)$ for some $a>1$, then Equation <a href="#eq:green-poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:green-poisson">[eq:green-poisson]</a> passes to $r=1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-green"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-green</summary>

*Proof.* The identities are the ordinary planar Green identities with $\Delta=4\partial_z\partial_{\bar z}$. For the limiting assertion, $\log(1/|z|)$ belongs to every finite $L^b(\mathbb D)$, so Hölder’s inequality and dominated convergence apply to the right-hand side. Continuity gives convergence of the circle means. ◻

</details>

Sobolev regularity of a continuous map on $\overline\mathbb D$ is understood in finitely many target coordinate charts. The same convention applies to sections of a pulled-back bundle. When $p>2$, the embedding $W^{1,p}(\mathbb D)\hookrightarrow C^0(\overline\mathbb D)$, its local versions, and the multiplication estimate make $W^{1,p}$ an algebra. Smooth functions of finitely many Sobolev coefficients are again Sobolev when their values stay in a compact subset of their domain. We use the corresponding smooth composition maps between these Banach spaces. For fiberwise holomorphic functions with uniform radii and Sobolev coefficient bounds, composition is holomorphic between the complex Banach spaces in question; the precise application is given in the variation construction.

The following table collects the notation used throughout the disc argument. The area and Poisson functionals use the fixed unit disc, with $A_{q,r}$ denoting the explicitly indicated truncation.

<div class="center">

| Notation             | Meaning                                                  |
|:---------------------|:---------------------------------------------------------|
| $k$                  | Fixed background Kähler metric on $X$                    |
| $h$                  | Auxiliary Kähler metric                                  |
| $R_h$                | $-dd^c\log\det h$, representing $-2\pi c_1(K_X)$         |
| $d(f)$               | Squared central derivative norm $k(f'(0),f'(0))$         |
| $A_q(f)$             | $\pi^{-1}\int_\mathbb Dq(f',f')\,\mathrm dS$             |
| $A_{q,r}(f)$         | The same area integral over $|z|<r$                      |
| $P_q(f)$             | $2\pi^{-1}\int_\mathbb D\log(1/|z|)q(f',f')\,\mathrm dS$ |
| $\langle u\rangle_r$ | Normalized mean of $u$ on $|z|=r$                        |

</div>

<a id="sec:extremal-discs"></a>
### Extremal discs and boundary regularity

Our goal is to maximize the disc functional and obtain enough boundary regularity to vary the whole disc. Hyperbolicity first gives compactness on interior subdiscs; an area penalty will control the boundary tail. Throughout this section, $k$ is the fixed Kähler metric on $X$. We write $|f'(z)|_k^2=k(f'(z),f'(z))$ and use the path distance of the associated Riemannian metric when discussing uniform convergence. The fixed numerical factor relating real and complex tangent norms will be absorbed into constants.

<a id="stmt-ex-brody"></a>
<div id="ex:brody" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:brody-derivative"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Derivative bound and normality). *There is a constant $D>0$, depending only on $(X,k)$, such that every holomorphic map $f\colon\mathbb D\to X$ satisfies $$d(f)=|f'(0)|_k^2\leq D.$$ Consequently, $$|f'(z)|_k\leq\frac{\sqrt D}{1-|z|},\qquad z\in\mathbb D.
 \label{eq:brody-derivative}$$ Every sequence of such maps has a subsequence converging uniformly on compact subsets of $\mathbb D$ to a holomorphic map into $X$. In local target coordinates, all derivatives converge uniformly on smaller compact subsets as well.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-brody"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-brody</summary>

*Proof.* We give Brody’s rescaling argument in the normalization needed here (Brody 1978) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Brody1978](#ref-Brody1978)]. Suppose first that there are maps $f_j\colon\mathbb D\to X$ with $|f_j'(0)|_k\to\infty$. Choose $z_j$ at which the continuous function $$z\longmapsto (1/2-|z|)|f_j'(z)|_k$$ attains its maximum on $\{|z|\leq1/2\}$. For all sufficiently large $j$ this maximum is positive, so $|z_j|<1/2$. Put $$\lambda_j=|f_j'(z_j)|_k,
 \qquad R_j=(1/2-|z_j|)\lambda_j,
 \qquad g_j(w)=f_j(z_j+w/\lambda_j).$$ The map $g_j$ is defined on $\{|w|<R_j\}$, and $$R_j\geq\tfrac12|f_j'(0)|_k\longrightarrow\infty,
 \qquad |g_j'(0)|_k=1.$$ If $|w|<R_j/2$, then $$1/2-|z_j+w/\lambda_j|
 \geq\tfrac12(1/2-|z_j|).$$ The maximizing property of $z_j$ therefore gives $|g_j'(w)|_k\leq2$ on this smaller disc.

For every fixed radius these maps are consequently equicontinuous and take values in the compact metric space $X$. The Arzelà–Ascoli theorem and a diagonal subsequence give a locally uniform limit $g\colon\mathbb C\to X$. To verify holomorphy, choose a point of the source and a target coordinate chart containing its image under $g$. On a sufficiently small source neighborhood, the limit and all sufficiently late maps take values in that chart. Their coordinate representations converge uniformly on smaller neighborhoods. The usual theorem on uniform limits of holomorphic functions, followed by the Cauchy integral formula for derivatives, shows that $g$ is holomorphic and $|g'(0)|_k=1$. This contradicts the hypothesis on entire maps. The central derivative bound follows.

For $z\in\mathbb D$, apply this bound to $w\mapsto f(z+(1-|z|)w)$. Its central derivative is $(1-|z|)f'(z)$, proving Equation <a href="#eq:brody-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:brody-derivative">[eq:brody-derivative]</a>. The estimate gives equicontinuity on every compact source disc. A second application of Arzelà–Ascoli and the same coordinate argument proves normality and convergence of derivatives. Increasing $D$ if necessary makes it strictly positive. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:disc-functional"></a>
We next fix a point $x\in X$, a smooth Kähler form $h$, a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form $q$, and a number $\delta>0$. All constants in the next two results may depend on these choices. Finite area can be measured using either $h$ or $k$, since the two metrics are uniformly comparable on $X$. We consider $$J_{h,q,\delta}(f)=d(f)+P_q(f)-\delta A_h(f)
 \label{eq:disc-functional}$$ on the finite-area holomorphic discs with $f(0)=x$.

<a id="stmt-ex-maximizer"></a>
<div id="ex:maximizer" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (Existence of a maximizing disc). *The functional in Equation <a href="#eq:disc-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:disc-functional">[eq:disc-functional]</a> has finite supremum, attained by a finite-area holomorphic disc centered at $x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-maximizer"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-maximizer</summary>

<a id="eq:maximizing-area-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Choose $C_q\geq0$ such that $$|q(v,v)|\leq C_q h(v,v)$$ for every tangent vector $v$. Select $r_0\in[1/2,1)$ so close to $1$ that $2C_q\log(1/r_0)\leq\delta/2$. For a disc $f$ write $$a_f(z)=h(f'(z),f'(z)),\qquad b_f(z)=q(f'(z),f'(z)).$$ On the outer annulus $r_0<|z|<1$, the function $$T_f(z)=\delta a_f(z)-2\log(1/|z|)b_f(z)$$ satisfies <a id="eq:negative-tail-density"></a>$$T_f(z)\geq\frac\delta2 a_f(z)\geq0.
 \label{eq:negative-tail-density}$$ On the inner disc, Lemma <a href="#ex:brody" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:brody">3</a> and comparison of $h$ with $k$ give a uniform bound for $a_f$ and $|b_f|$. Since $\log(1/|z|)$ is integrable there, there is a constant $K$ independent of $f$ such that $$\left|\frac1\pi\int_{|z|<r_0}
 \bigl(2\log(1/|z|)b_f(z)-\delta a_f(z)\bigr)\,dS\right|
 \leq K.$$ Consequently, $$J_{h,q,\delta}(f)
 \leq D+K-\frac\delta{2\pi}\int_{r_0<|z|<1}a_f\,dS.
 \label{eq:maximizing-area-bound}$$ In particular, the supremum is finite. It is nonnegative because the constant disc has value zero.

Choose a maximizing sequence $(f_j)$ with $J_{h,q,\delta}(f_j)\geq-1$. Equation <a href="#eq:maximizing-area-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximizing-area-bound">[eq:maximizing-area-bound]</a> bounds its outer-annulus areas uniformly, and the compact-interior derivative estimate bounds its inner areas. Thus $$\sup_j A_h(f_j)<\infty.$$ By Lemma <a href="#ex:brody" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:brody">3</a>, after passing to a subsequence the maps converge locally uniformly to a holomorphic disc $f$ centered at $x$. Derivative convergence on compact subsets gives pointwise convergence of $a_{f_j}$ and $b_{f_j}$. Fatou’s lemma gives $A_h(f)\leq\liminf_j A_h(f_j)<\infty$.

We verify the required upper semicontinuity of $J$. Its central term converges by derivative convergence. Its inner-disc integral converges by dominated convergence, using the uniform bound above together with the integrable logarithmic weight. On the outer annulus, the nonnegative functions $T_{f_j}$ converge pointwise to $T_f$, so Fatou’s lemma yields $$-\frac1\pi\int_{r_0<|z|<1}T_f\,dS
 \geq\limsup_j
 \left(-\frac1\pi\int_{r_0<|z|<1}T_{f_j}\,dS\right).$$ All these integrals are finite. Indeed, finite area controls the unweighted integrals and the logarithmic weight is bounded on the outer annulus. Combining the three terms proves $$J_{h,q,\delta}(f)\geq\limsup_jJ_{h,q,\delta}(f_j),$$ which proves attainment. ◻

</details>

Recall that $A_{q,s}(f)$ is the area integral restricted to $|z|<s$. The form $q$ need not be positive, so this quantity can have either sign. Nevertheless, $|q|\leq C_qh$ implies that $A_{q,s}(f)$ is bounded in $s$ and tends to $A_q(f)$ as $s$ tends to $1$. Maximality now improves the finite area of the disc to a quantitative tail estimate. This is the additional control needed at the boundary.

<a id="stmt-ex-dilation"></a>
<div id="ex:dilation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:tail"></a>
<a id="eq:extremal-area-tail"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Dilation and area tails). *If $f$ is a maximizer in Proposition <a href="#ex:maximizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:maximizer">4</a>, then, for $0<r<1$, $$\begin{split}
 0\leq\delta\bigl(A_h(f)-A_{h,r}(f)\bigr)
 &\leq(1-r^2)d(f)
       +2\int_r^1 A_{q,s}(f)\,\frac{ds}{s}.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:tail}$$ In particular, $$A_q(f)\geq-d(f),\qquad
 A_k(f)-A_{k,r}(f)\leq C_f(1-r)\quad(1/2\leq r<1)
 \label{eq:extremal-area-tail}$$ for some finite constant $C_f$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-dilation"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-dilation</summary>

*Proof.* Let $f_r(z)=f(rz)$. A change of variables gives $$d(f_r)=r^2d(f),\qquad
 A_h(f_r)=A_{h,r}(f),\qquad
 A_{q,s}(f_r)=A_{q,rs}(f).$$ The identity $\log(1/|z|)=\int_{|z|}^1ds/s$ and Fubini’s theorem give $$P_q(f)=2\int_0^1 A_{q,s}(f)\,\frac{ds}{s}.$$ Fubini is valid also for this signed integrand: near the origin the coordinate derivatives of $f$ are bounded, while away from the origin the logarithmic weight is bounded and $|q(f',f')|$ is controlled by the finite $h$-area. Applying the same identity to $f_r$ gives <a id="eq:dilation-poisson"></a>$$P_q(f)-P_q(f_r)
   =2\int_r^1 A_{q,s}(f)\,\frac{ds}{s}.
 \label{eq:dilation-poisson}$$ Since $f_r$ has the same center and finite area, maximality gives $J_{h,q,\delta}(f)\geq J_{h,q,\delta}(f_r)$. Substituting the three identities proves Equation <a href="#eq:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail">[eq:tail]</a>; its leftmost inequality uses the positivity of $h$.

Divide the rightmost expression in Equation <a href="#eq:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail">[eq:tail]</a> by $1-r$. As $r\uparrow1$, it tends to $2d(f)+2A_q(f)$. It is nonnegative for every $r$, and hence $A_q(f)\geq-d(f)$. For $r\geq1/2$, the original right-hand side of Equation <a href="#eq:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail">[eq:tail]</a> is at most $$2d(f)(1-r)+4C_qA_h(f)(1-r).$$ This bounds the $h$-area tail by a constant times $1-r$. Uniform comparison of $k$ and $h$ then gives the second assertion in Equation <a href="#eq:extremal-area-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extremal-area-tail">[eq:extremal-area-tail]</a>. ◻

</details>

The tail estimate supplies the last step of this section: continuity on the closed disc and a Sobolev exponent strictly above two. These are precisely the hypotheses used to construct the frames and variations in Section <a href="#var:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:section">4</a>.

<a id="stmt-ex-regularity"></a>
<div id="ex:regularity" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:boundary-derivative"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Boundary regularity of maximizing discs). *Every disc maximizing the functional in Equation <a href="#eq:disc-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:disc-functional">[eq:disc-functional]</a> extends continuously to $\overline\mathbb D$ and belongs to $W^{1,p}(\mathbb D,X)$ for every $2<p<4$. In particular, it has continuous $W^{1,3}$ regularity up to the boundary. More precisely, there is a constant $C_f$ such that $$|f'(z)|_k\leq C_f(1-|z|)^{-1/2}
 \label{eq:boundary-derivative}$$ for $z$ sufficiently close to $\partial\mathbb D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-regularity</summary>

*Proof.* We first make precise the uniform choice of target charts for small source discs. Choose finitely many nested coordinate neighborhoods $$V_\alpha\Subset W_\alpha\Subset U_\alpha$$ such that the $V_\alpha$ cover $X$ and each $U_\alpha$ is a holomorphic coordinate chart. By compactness, there is a number $\rho>0$ such that if $y\in V_\alpha$, its metric ball of radius $\rho$ is contained in $W_\alpha$. On each $\overline{W_\alpha}$, the metric $k$ and the Euclidean coordinate metric are uniformly comparable; one comparison constant suffices for this finite collection.

For $z\in\mathbb D$ put $t=1-|z|$. Let $0<c<1/4$, to be fixed independently of $z$ and $f$. For $w\in B(z,ct)$, the straight segment from $z$ to $w$ stays at source distance at least $(1-c)t$ from $\partial\mathbb D$. Equation <a href="#eq:brody-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:brody-derivative">[eq:brody-derivative]</a> therefore bounds the length of its image by $$C\sqrt D\,\frac{c}{1-c},$$ where $C$ accounts only for the convention relating real and complex tangent norms. Choose $c$ small enough that this bound is less than $\rho$. Selecting $\alpha$ with $f(z)\in V_\alpha$, we obtain $$f\bigl(B(z,ct)\bigr)\subset W_\alpha.$$

In these coordinates the derivative of $f$ is a vector of holomorphic functions. Its squared Euclidean norm is subharmonic. The mean-value inequality and the uniform metric comparisons give $$|f'(z)|_k^2
 \leq\frac{C_1}{t^2}
       \int_{B(z,ct)}|f'(w)|_k^2\,dS(w).$$ Every point of $B(z,ct)$ has modulus greater than $1-(1+c)t$. For $t$ sufficiently small, the area-tail estimate in Equation <a href="#eq:extremal-area-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extremal-area-tail">[eq:extremal-area-tail]</a> consequently gives $$\int_{B(z,ct)}|f'(w)|_k^2\,dS(w)
 \leq\pi\bigl(A_k(f)-A_{k,1-(1+c)t}(f)\bigr)
 \leq C_2t.$$ This proves Equation <a href="#eq:boundary-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-derivative">[eq:boundary-derivative]</a>.

For $r<s<1$ sufficiently close to $1$ and $\zeta\in\partial\mathbb D$, integration along the radial segment gives $$\operatorname{dist}_k\bigl(f(r\zeta),f(s\zeta)\bigr)
 \leq C_3\int_r^s(1-u)^{-1/2}\,du
 \leq2C_3\sqrt{1-r}.$$ The estimate is uniform in $\zeta$. Since $X$ is complete, each radial path has a limit, and the continuous maps $\zeta\mapsto f(r\zeta)$ converge uniformly as $r\uparrow1$. Their limit is therefore continuous on $\partial\mathbb D$. The same uniform estimate, together with continuity of this boundary map, proves that the resulting extension of $f$ is continuous on all of $\overline\mathbb D$.

It remains to improve the integrability of the derivative. For all sufficiently large integers $j$, let $$E_j=\{z\in\mathbb D:2^{-j-1}<1-|z|<2^{-j}\}.$$ The area-tail estimate and Equation <a href="#eq:boundary-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-derivative">[eq:boundary-derivative]</a> imply $$\int_{E_j}|f'|_k^2\,dS\leq C_4\,2^{-j},
 \qquad
 \sup_{E_j}|f'|_k\leq C_5\,2^{j/2}.$$ Thus, for $2<p<4$, $$\begin{split}
 \int_{E_j}|f'|_k^p\,dS
 &\leq\bigl(\sup_{E_j}|f'|_k\bigr)^{p-2}
              \int_{E_j}|f'|_k^2\,dS\\
 &\leq C_p\,2^{-j(4-p)/2}.
 \end{split}$$ This is summable in $j$. On the remaining compact interior disc the derivative is bounded, so $|f'|_k\in L^p(\mathbb D)$.

For completeness, the asserted Sobolev regularity is understood in local target coordinates. Continuity on $\overline\mathbb D$ allows a finite cover of the closed source disc by small neighborhoods whose images lie in relatively compact target charts. In each such chart, the coordinate functions are bounded, are smooth in the source interior, and have first derivatives in $L^p$ by the estimate just proved and metric comparison. These classical derivatives are their weak derivatives, as is seen by testing against compactly supported smooth functions in the source interior. Hence the coordinate functions lie in $W^{1,p}$ up to the boundary, with the continuous boundary values already constructed. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 7*. The constants in Proposition <a href="#ex:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:regularity">6</a> need not be uniform in $h$, $q$, or $\delta$. What will be used is the regularity of each individual maximizing disc, to construct variations with unrestricted boundary values. The constant $D$ in Lemma <a href="#ex:brody" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:brody">3</a>, in contrast, depends only on the fixed metric $k$ and the compact manifold $X$.

</div>

<a id="var:section"></a>
### Boundary frames and centered holomorphic variations

We establish the two analytic facts needed to vary the maximizing disc. Throughout this section, let $$f\colon\overline\mathbb D\longrightarrow X$$ be continuous, holomorphic on $\mathbb D$, and of class $W^{1,p}$ for some $p>2$, with the coordinate convention of Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. The maximizers supplied by Proposition <a href="#ex:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:regularity">6</a> satisfy these assumptions. No extension of $f$ across $\partial\mathbb D$ is assumed.

We use two elementary consequences of $p>2$: the embedding $W^{1,p}(\mathbb D)\subset C^0(\overline\mathbb D)$ and the fact that $W^{1,p}(\mathbb D)$ is a Banach algebra under pointwise multiplication. In particular, a continuous, pointwise invertible $W^{1,p}$ matrix on $\overline\mathbb D$ has a $W^{1,p}$ inverse.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### A holomorphic frame regular at the boundary

We first trivialize the pulled-back tangent bundle while retaining the boundary Sobolev regularity of $f$. We will then change this frame so that its boundary values are orthonormal for $h$.

<a id="stmt-var-initial-frame"></a>
<div id="var:initial-frame" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *The bundle $f^*T^{1,0}X$ admits a holomorphic frame $e$ on $\mathbb D$ which extends to a continuous, nonsingular $W^{1,p}$ frame on $\overline\mathbb D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-initial-frame"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-initial-frame</summary>

*Proof.* The continuous pullback bundle over $\overline\mathbb D$ is topologically trivial. Its coordinate transition matrices are $W^{1,p}$: they are smooth functions of $f$ with bounded derivatives on the compact subsets of the target charts in use. Start with a continuous frame, approximate its coefficients uniformly in finitely many local frames, and combine the approximations with a smooth partition of unity on the source. This gives a $W^{1,p}$ frame $v$ uniformly close to the original one; sufficiently close approximation preserves its pointwise independence.

<a id="var:cauchy-estimates"></a>
In the frame $v$, the Dolbeault operator on the open disc has the form $$\bar\partial+a,
 \qquad a\in L^p(\mathbb D,\operatorname{Mat}_n(\mathbb C)),$$ where we identify a $(0,1)$-form with its coefficient. Extend $a$ by zero to a disc $\mathbb D_R=\{|z|<R\}$ with $R>1$. On a sufficiently small disc $U\subset\mathbb D_R$ of radius $r$, solve <a id="var:local-gauge"></a>$$\label{var:local-gauge}
 \bar\partial U_0=-aU_0.$$ Here and below the same notation for a matrix equation is understood entrywise. For completeness, the solid Cauchy transform $$T_Ug(z)=\frac1\pi\int_U\frac{g(\zeta)}{z-\zeta}\,\mathrm dS(\zeta)$$ satisfies $\bar\partial T_Ug=g$ weakly in $U$. Hölder’s inequality for its kernel and the $L^p$ estimate for the planar Cauchy singular integral (Sukhov and Tumanov 2016, sec. 4.1 of the authors’ preprint) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[SukhovTumanov2016](#ref-SukhovTumanov2016)] give $$\label{var:cauchy-estimates}
 \|T_Ug\|_{L^\infty(U)}
       \le C_p r^{1-2/p}\|g\|_{L^p(U)},
 \qquad
 \|T_Ug\|_{W^{1,p}(U)}\le C_{p,r}\|g\|_{L^p(U)}.$$ Thus $U_0=I-T_U(aU_0)$ is solved by contraction in the sup norm when $r$ is small. The radius can also be chosen so that the solution stays within distance $1/2$ of $I$. It is therefore invertible and, by Equation <a href="#var:cauchy-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:cauchy-estimates">[var:cauchy-estimates]</a>, belongs to $W^{1,p}$.

For any two such solutions $U_i,U_j$, the transition matrix $U_i^{-1}U_j$ satisfies $\bar\partial(U_i^{-1}U_j)=0$ weakly on the overlap. It is consequently holomorphic there. These transitions define a holomorphic vector bundle on $\mathbb D_R$. A holomorphic vector bundle on a disc is holomorphically trivial, by the Oka–Grauert principle (Grauert 1958; Forstnerič and Prezelj 2000) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Grauert1958](#ref-Grauert1958), [ForstnericPrezelj2000](#ref-ForstnericPrezelj2000)]. Choose a global holomorphic frame of this bundle. Relative to its local frames $U_i$, its coefficients are holomorphic; relative to the original trivial bundle they are therefore $W^{1,p}$ on compact subsets of $\mathbb D_R$. In particular the resulting matrix $U$ is continuous, invertible, and $W^{1,p}$ on $\overline\mathbb D$, and satisfies $\bar\partial U=-aU$ on $\mathbb D$. The frame $e=vU$ has all the required properties. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-var-frame"></a>
<div id="var:frame" class="proposition">

<a id="var:boundary-unitary"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Boundary-unitary frame). *Let $h$ be a smooth Hermitian metric on $X$. There exist a number $p_0$ with $2<p_0\le p$ and a holomorphic frame $B=(b_1,\ldots,b_n)$ of $f^*T^{1,0}X$ which is continuous, nonsingular, and $W^{1,p_0}$ on $\overline\mathbb D$, such that $$\label{var:boundary-unitary}
 B(z)^*h(f(z))B(z)=I,
 \qquad z\in\partial\mathbb D.$$*

</div>

The factorization step belongs to the matrix spectral-factorization theory of Wiener–Masani and Helson–Lowdenslager (Wiener and Masani 1957; Helson and Lowdenslager 1958) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[WienerMasani1957](#ref-WienerMasani1957), [HelsonLowdenslager1958](#ref-HelsonLowdenslager1958)]. We give the weighted Hardy-space construction and establish the boundary Sobolev regularity required for our variations.

<details id="proof-stmt-var-frame"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-frame</summary>

<a id="var:metric-bounds"></a>
*Proof.* Choose $e$ by Lemma <a href="#var:initial-frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:initial-frame">8</a> and set $$M(z)=e(z)^*h(f(z))e(z).$$ This is a continuous, positive definite $W^{1,p}$ matrix on the closed disc. In particular, there are constants $0<m\le M_0<\infty$ such that $$\label{var:metric-bounds}
 mI\le M(z)\le M_0I
 \quad\hbox{on }\overline\mathbb D.$$

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### The Hardy-space factorization.

Write $H^2=H^2(\partial\mathbb D,\mathbb C^n)$ for the vector Hardy space with nonnegative Fourier modes, and put $\mathrm d\sigma=\mathrm d\theta/(2\pi)$. Equip the same vector space with the equivalent Hilbert norm $$\|u\|_M^2=\int_{\partial\mathbb D}u^*Mu\,\mathrm d\sigma.$$ Multiplication by $z$ is an isometry, and its closed image $zH^2$ has codimension $n$. Let $s_1,\ldots,s_n$ be an orthonormal basis of $W=H^2\ominus_M zH^2$ and form the matrix $S=(s_1,\ldots,s_n)$. The vectors $z^j s_a$, with $j\ge0$ and $1\le a\le n$, are an orthonormal basis. Indeed, iterating $H^2=W\oplus_M zH^2$ shows that the orthogonal complement of their span is contained in every $z^jH^2$; the intersection of these spaces is zero.

<a id="var:hardy-factor"></a>
Their orthogonality identifies all Fourier coefficients of the $L^1$ matrix $S^*MS$, and gives $$\label{var:hardy-factor}
 S^*MS=I\quad\hbox{almost everywhere on }\partial\mathbb D.$$ Equation <a href="#var:metric-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:metric-bounds">[var:metric-bounds]</a> then bounds the boundary values of $S$ in $L^\infty$. Its holomorphic Hardy extension is bounded on $\mathbb D$, as follows entrywise from the Poisson integral. It is invertible at every point $\zeta\in\mathbb D$: evaluation at $\zeta$ is a continuous surjection from $H^2$ onto $\mathbb C^n$, whereas the evaluations of all $z^j s_a$ lie in the range of $S(\zeta)$. Their dense span forces that range to be $\mathbb C^n$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### Regularity of the boundary factor.

<a id="var:trace-difference"></a>
The factor constructed so far has only almost-everywhere boundary values. We now obtain continuity and a Sobolev exponent above two, which will also give nonsingularity at every boundary point. We first record the fractional regularity supplied by $M\in W^{1,p}$. If $M_\theta(e^{i\alpha})=M(e^{i(\alpha+\theta)})$, then $$\label{var:trace-difference}
 \|M_\theta-M\|_{L^p(\partial\mathbb D)}
       \le C|\theta|^{1-1/p},\qquad |\theta|\le\tfrac14.$$ One can see this directly, without a trace theorem. Set $t=|\theta|$ and choose $\rho\in[1-2t,1-t]$ such that the angular $L^p$ norm of $\partial_\alpha M(\rho e^{i\alpha})$ is at most $Ct^{-1/p}\|M\|_{W^{1,p}}$. Radial integration and Hölder’s inequality bound $\|M|_{\partial\mathbb D}-M(\rho\,\cdot)\|_{L^p}$ by $Ct^{1-1/p}\|M\|_{W^{1,p}}$. At radius $\rho$, angular integration gives the same bound for translation through $\theta$. The triangle inequality proves Equation <a href="#var:trace-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:trace-difference">[var:trace-difference]</a>; the argument for Sobolev functions follows by approximation, with the continuous boundary values identifying the trace.

Let $H^s(S^1)$ now denote the fractional Sobolev space on the circle, defined by square summability of Fourier coefficients with weight $1+|l|^{2s}$. This differs from the Hardy-space notation $H^2$ above. It follows that $M|_{\partial\mathbb D}\in H^s(S^1)$ for every <a id="var:s-choice"></a>$$\label{var:s-choice}
 \frac12<s<1-\frac1p.$$ Indeed, on a circle of finite measure the $L^p$ bound controls the $L^2$ bound, and integration of the squared rotation differences against $|\theta|^{-1-2s}\,\mathrm d\theta$ is finite. Parseval’s identity identifies this condition with $\sum_{l\in\mathbb Z}(1+|l|^{2s})|M_l|^2<\infty$.

Let $\Pi$ denote the unweighted orthogonal Hardy projection. The Toeplitz operator $$T_Mv=\Pi(Mv),\qquad T_M\colon H^2\longrightarrow H^2,$$ is bounded, self-adjoint, and satisfies $\langle T_Mv,v\rangle\ge m\|v\|_2^2$. Consequently it has a bounded inverse: its range is closed by the lower bound and dense because its orthogonal complement is the kernel of its adjoint. For a column $v$ of $S$, membership in $W$ says precisely that $T_Mv$ is a constant vector. Rotating this identity and subtracting it from the original one yields $$T_M(v_\theta-v)=\Pi\bigl((M-M_\theta)v_\theta\bigr).$$ The boundedness of $v$ and $T_M^{-1}$ gives <a id="var:factor-difference"></a>$$\label{var:factor-difference}
 \|v_\theta-v\|_2
 \le C\|(M_\theta-M)v_\theta\|_2
 \le C\|M_\theta-M\|_2.$$ The same rotation characterization therefore gives $v\in H^s$.

Write $v(z)=\sum_{l\ge0}v_lz^l$. Since $s>1/2$, Cauchy–Schwarz shows that this series converges absolutely and uniformly on $\overline\mathbb D$. Thus $v$ is continuous there. More precisely, for $1/2<r<1$, Parseval’s identity and Cauchy–Schwarz give <a id="var:factor-l2"></a><a id="var:factor-sup"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|v'(r\,\cdot)\|_2^2
 &\le C(1-r)^{2s-2}
       \sum_{l\ge1}l^{2s}|v_l|^2,
       \label{var:factor-l2}\\
 \|v'(r\,\cdot)\|_\infty
 &\le C(1-r)^{s-3/2}
       \left(\sum_{l\ge1}l^{2s}|v_l|^2\right)^{1/2}.
       \label{var:factor-sup}
\end{aligned}$$ For any $p_0>2$, interpolation of these two estimates bounds $\|v'(r\,\cdot)\|_{p_0}^{p_0}$ by a constant times $$(1-r)^{(s-3/2)(p_0-2)+2s-2}
       =(1-r)^{p_0(s-3/2)+1}.$$ This is integrable in $r$ provided <a id="var:p0-choice"></a>$$\label{var:p0-choice}
 2<p_0<\frac{2}{3/2-s}.$$ The interval is nonempty because $s>1/2$, and we choose $p_0$ also at most $p$. It follows that $S\in W^{1,p_0}(\mathbb D)$.

By continuity, Equation <a href="#var:hardy-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:hardy-factor">[var:hardy-factor]</a> now holds at every boundary point. It implies that $S$ is invertible there as well as in the interior. Compactness gives uniform nonsingularity on $\overline\mathbb D$. Finally $B=eS$ is a continuous, nonsingular, $W^{1,p_0}$ holomorphic frame and satisfies Equation <a href="#var:boundary-unitary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:boundary-unitary">[var:boundary-unitary]</a>. Notice that uniform nonsingularity was obtained after boundary regularity; it was not assumed in the Hardy-space argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Integrating the infinitesimal variations

The boundary-unitary frame provides sections $zb_j$ vanishing at the center. To use maximality, these sections must be derivatives of admissible holomorphic discs. Sprays of analytic discs fixing an exceptional set are standard in disc-envelope theory (Drinovec Drnovšek and Forstnerič 2012, Definition 2.1 and Lemma 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[DrinovecForstneric2012](#ref-DrinovecForstneric2012)]. The following construction records the prescribed derivatives and Sobolev parameter dependence required by the Hessian calculation.

<a id="stmt-var-spray"></a>
<div id="var:spray" class="proposition">

<a id="var:spray-identities"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Centered holomorphic variations). *Let $B$ and $p_0>2$ be as in Proposition <a href="#var:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:frame">9</a>, and set $x=f(0)$. For some $\eta>0$, there is a map $$F\colon\{t\in\mathbb C^n:|t|<\eta\}\times\overline\mathbb D\longrightarrow X$$ which is continuous on its domain and jointly holomorphic on $\{|t|<\eta\}\times\mathbb D$, and satisfies $$\label{var:spray-identities}
 F(0,z)=f(z),\qquad F(t,0)=x,\qquad
 \frac{\partial F}{\partial t_j}(0,z)=z b_j(z).$$ In fixed local target coordinates covering the graph of $f$, the maps $t\mapsto F(t,\cdot)$ are holomorphic with values in $W^{1,p_0}$ on the corresponding source patches. In particular every $F(t,\cdot)$ is a finite-area holomorphic disc centered at $x$.*

</div>

The proof first gives the graph of $f$ fiberwise holomorphic coordinates with Sobolev dependence on the source. The resulting coordinate maps need not be holomorphic in the source; we correct that defect by a nonlinear $\bar\partial$ equation. Subtracting the central value in its right inverse preserves the prescribed center.

<details id="proof-stmt-var-spray"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-spray</summary>

*Proof.* **Fiber coordinates.** Choose a frame $e$ from Lemma <a href="#var:initial-frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:initial-frame">8</a> and write $B=eS$. The matrix $S=e^{-1}B$ is holomorphic on $\mathbb D$ and belongs to $W^{1,p_0}$ on $\overline\mathbb D$. We first construct fiber coordinates near the graph of $f$. Choose a finite cover of $\overline\mathbb D$ by relatively open sets $U_i$ and target charts $\phi_i\colon V_i\longrightarrow\mathbb C^n$ so that $f(\overline U_i)$ is a compact subset of $V_i$, after shrinking the source sets as necessary. Let $\rho_i$ be a smooth partition of unity on the closed disc subordinate to these sets. In the $i$th chart put $$A_i(z)=D\phi_i(f(z))e(z),\qquad
 C_i(z,y)=A_i(z)^{-1}\bigl(\phi_i(y)-\phi_i(f(z))\bigr).$$ For $z\in U_i$, the matrix $A_i$ is holomorphic in the interior, continuous and invertible up to the boundary, and of class $W^{1,p_0}$. The map $C_i$ is holomorphic in $y$ and, for fixed admissible $y$, holomorphic in the interior source variable $z$. On a neighborhood of the graph of $f$ define <a id="var:forward-coordinates"></a>$$\label{var:forward-coordinates}
 \Phi(z,y)=\sum_i\rho_i(z)C_i(z,y).$$ Each term is used only where its target chart is defined; the compact support of $\rho_i$ inside the corresponding source patch makes extension by zero harmless. These neighborhoods can be chosen uniformly about the graph. More precisely, on sufficiently small source patches all indices whose supports meet the patch have their charts defined on one common target neighborhood of its image under $f$. This follows from finiteness of the cover and subordination of the supports. By construction, <a id="var:coordinate-normalization"></a>$$\label{var:coordinate-normalization}
 \Phi(z,f(z))=0,
 \qquad D_y\Phi(z,f(z))\,e(z)=I.$$

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Uniform inverses and their Sobolev dependence.

<a id="var:inverse-normalization"></a>
Work on one of these smaller source patches, with fixed target chart $\psi$, and put $c(z)=\psi(f(z))$ and $e_\psi(z)=D\psi(f(z))e(z)$. Normalize the forward map by $$K_z(v)=\Phi\bigl(z,\psi^{-1}(c(z)+e_\psi(z)v)\bigr).$$ Then $K_z(0)=0$ and $D_vK_z(0)=I$. Continuity of all fiber derivatives and compactness allow a common $R>0$ such that the maps are defined on $|v|\le R$ and $$\|D_vK_z(v)-I\|\le\tfrac14.$$ For $|\xi|<R/2$, the equation $$v=\xi-\bigl(K_z(v)-v\bigr)$$ is a contraction of the closed ball of radius $R$ into itself. Its solution $V(z,\xi)$ is continuous in $(z,\xi)$ and holomorphic in $\xi$, as follows from its uniformly convergent contraction iterations. Define $E$ by $\psi(E(z,\xi))=c(z)+e_\psi(z)V(z,\xi)$. The local inverses agree near $\xi=0$ by uniqueness and then throughout their common fiber domain by holomorphy. Taking the minimum of the finitely many radii gives a common fiber neighborhood, with $$\Phi(z,E(z,\xi))=\xi,$$ and $$\label{var:inverse-normalization}
 E(z,0)=f(z),\qquad E_\xi(z,0)=e(z).$$

<a id="var:inverse-sobolev-bound"></a>
We next obtain a Sobolev estimate uniform in the fiber parameter. The chart formula for $K_z$ depends smoothly on the finite coefficient list $$a(z)=\bigl(\rho_i(z),A_i(z)^{-1},\phi_i(f(z)),
                 c(z),e_\psi(z)\bigr)_i.$$ All these coefficients are $W^{1,p_0}$ with compact range. Fix a smaller closed fiber polydisc inside $|\xi|<R/2$. The strict bounds in the contraction argument persist when the coefficient list varies in a sufficiently small neighborhood of its compact range. The finite-dimensional implicit function theorem, with the coefficients regarded as real parameters, therefore makes $V$ a smooth function of that list and $\xi$, holomorphic in $\xi$. After shrinking this coefficient neighborhood, its first coefficient derivatives are uniformly bounded on the fixed fiber polydisc. The same is true for the coordinate inverse $E_\psi(z,\xi):=\psi(E(z,\xi))=c(z)+e_\psi(z)V(z,\xi)$. The Sobolev chain rule now shows that $E_\psi(\cdot,\xi)$ is $W^{1,p_0}$, with its weak source derivatives bounded by a single $L^{p_0}$ function independent of $\xi$. In particular, for some $b\in L^{p_0}$ on the source patch, $$\label{var:inverse-sobolev-bound}
 |E_\psi(z,\xi)|\le C,
 \qquad |\nabla_zE_\psi(z,\xi)|\le C b(z)
 \quad\hbox{for almost every }z,$$ uniformly for $\xi$ in that smaller polydisc. One may take $b$ to be a constant plus the sum of the absolute values of the first weak derivatives of the finite coefficient list.
